### [A summary of Condensation and its relation to Natural Latents](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/agw7HhW4cWjADpBgo/a-summary-of-condensation-and-its-relation-to-natural)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1>Short summary of Condensation</h1><p>Condensation is a theory of concepts by Sam Eisenstat. The paper can be read <a href="https://openreview.net/pdf?id=HwKFJ3odui">here</a>. Abram wrote <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BstHXPgQyfeNnLjjp/condensation">a review</a> on Lesswrong, and a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2x9yatKKTRMabQAWq/condensation-and-relevance">followup</a>. The paper is very much worth reading, and can be skimmed to just understand the motivation if you’re time constrained.</p><p>What is a concept? One rough pointer is "the little packets of information that are the <i>meaning</i> of a word or phrase". Another is the clusters found in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WBw8dDkAWohFjWQSk/the-cluster-structure-of-thingspace">the cluster structure of thingspace</a>. Maybe we could think of them as little chunks of a generative model that compose together to make complete model of a complex world.</p><p>It often seems to be the case that this kind of chunking is arbitrary, in the sense that there isn’t one way to chunk up a generative model. It just depends on the hypothesis class, prior and implementation details. But some concepts seem to be more objective than this, in that we would expect different agents with different implementation details to use the same concept. One question that Condensation answers is <i>“under what conditions can concepts be more objective?”</i>. The theory provides some assumptions and metrics that can be used to select among <strong>latent variable models (LVM)s</strong> to find ones that use more “objective”<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi2gd03a2czq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni2gd03a2czq">[1]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;concepts. If we make these assumptions on two agents’ latent variable models, it tells us when they will (and won’t) use the same latent variables.</p><p>This is very similar to the motivation for John Wentworth’s <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03780">Natural Latents</a>, as is noted in the Condensation paper, but so far no one has worked through the similarities between the two on a mathematical level. So the main goal of this post is to show some simple relationships between these two theories. A secondary goal is to work through an intuitive example of a non-trivial condensation, and this is where we’ll start.</p><h2>How does condensation work?</h2><p>There are multiple ways we could organize a generative model. We’ll call a “way of organizing a generative model” an LVM. Condensation is a method for sorting among these and finding ones that are also likely to be found by other agents who are doing the same thing.</p><p>If we have a set of random variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, the space of LVMs can be (very roughly) thought of as the space of Bayesian Networks whose leaves are the random variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Except if two latent variables are upstream of the same subset of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>s, we'll combine them into a single variable. So we can name all the latent variables&nbsp;(<span class="math-tex"></span>)&nbsp;with the subscript indicating which variables they contribute to.</p><p>For example, for variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, an LVM might look like this:</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/agw7HhW4cWjADpBgo/hayozt8r1sfhgmobwrgg" /></figure><p>There is a&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variable for every subset of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variables, but under normal circumstances many of these will be constant random variables, so we can ignore them (they are omitted from the diagram to give a cleaner example).</p><p>The central metric of condensation is the conditioned score&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, which is a score for every&nbsp;variable&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in an LVM. The idea is that if two LVMs minimize every&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;simultaneously, they end up “sharing the same concepts” (in a certain sense).</p><h3>Example application</h3><p>A simple example where the latent variables are more “objective” is in clustering problems. The “objectively correct” latent variables are cluster parameters. If some clusters share similarities, then we have an “objectively correct” hierarchy of clusters. Take a second to intuitively understand the generating process of this data:</p><p><img alt="clustering_hierarchical_discrete.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772549712/lexical_client_uploads/yn0auhntnvfjzxriaxud.gif" /></p><p>We start with access to a distribution over a set of observation variables. In this example, we have a sequence of 36 2D observations.</p><p>For future use, we will calculate the joint entropy of some (carefully chosen) groups of these variables:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><p><img alt="entropy_diagram.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772550046/lexical_client_uploads/gzcyamc4wcgb0mqmztgf.png" /></p><p>As you can see if you watch the animation for a while, there is a hierarchical generating process behind this data. There are always four clusters, divided into two groups. Within each cluster, each sample is independent of the others. In each group, the cluster means are relatively close to each other. The shape of each cluster is always roughly the same as the shape of each other cluster.</p><p>We have lots of options for modelling this. We could fit an autoregressive sequence model such that it outputs a distribution and samples from it at every timestep. This would look like:</p><p>&nbsp;</p><figure class="image"><img alt="bayes_net_diagram (Edited 2).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772550643/lexical_client_uploads/pcmjw5maadxfxxln8lbh.png" /><figcaption>Every node has all previous nodes as its parents. Each is labeled with its entropy (conditioned on all parents). All entropies are approximate (and several are underestimated here).</figcaption><figcaption>All entropies are approximations estimated by sampling</figcaption></figure><p>Each node is labelled with its conditional entropy given its parents. We can use these to see how well this model does according to the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;scores. To work out a score&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, we look at a subset of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variables and add up the conditional entropy of every&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variable that contributes to that set. We then compare that to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will always be bigger than&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, and if they’re close together the score is good. If we try&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for the autoregressive model, we get&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. This is the same as&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, so the score is perfect at&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>In contrast, if we try&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, we get&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. This is far higher than&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, so the score is bad at&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Similarly,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, and most other sets have very bad scores.</p><p>So let’s look at another model structure. We could fit a generative model of all datapoints simultaneously using a GAN, VAE or Normalizing Flow. These generative models all involve sampling vector from a simple distribution and then passing it through a neural net to produce a sample from the learned distribution.</p><p><img alt="Generative model from noise(1).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772554480/lexical_client_uploads/zwcquykf6xunpaperlaf.png" /></p><p>This has a bad score for most choices of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, especially where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a small subset. The only good score is&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>In contrast, if we represent it as a Bayes net like this, we can see the structure more clearly:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="bayes_net_diagram.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772554557/lexical_client_uploads/aaiprdlpmjnmzsj8e8o8.png" /><figcaption><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a variable that contains the cluster center,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;contains the group center (a cluster containing two clusters), and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a 3 outcome random variable which chooses whether the covariance matrix of every&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is diagonal right, diagonal left, or round.</figcaption></figure><p>This has a much better score for most subsets. For example,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Compare this to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, it’s not too bad, much closer than previous models. For&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, it’s close to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>You might be wondering why these scores aren’t perfect, i.e. why does&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;not equal&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. The difference will be&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, which is how precisely we can nail down the parameters in&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;after observing&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. This should give you some intuition for when it’s possible to make a good condensation: When the relevant latent variables of the model can be precisely estimated from a single observation (or small subset of observations). This is why it works well for clustering parameters. The location parameter of a cluster (<span class="math-tex"></span>) can be fairly accurately estimated from just a single sample from that cluster.</p><p>What we can see is that minimizing the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;score everywhere requires that we divide up the noise variables in a way that attaches them only to exactly the observation variables that they influence. It also forces each&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variable to have the minimal possible entropy, so any unnecessary entropy will be removed. So we end up with this minimal latent variable model that is factored into meaningful chunks.</p><h2>Selected Results from Sam’s paper</h2><h3><strong>When is perfect condensation possible?</strong></h3><p>Theorem 5.10 in <a href="https://openreview.net/pdf?id=HwKFJ3odui">the paper</a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkc1foyh0oj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkc1foyh0oj">[2]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;gives us the necessary and sufficient properties for a perfect condensation (which is a condensation where every score&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is equal to&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>:</p><ol><li>Every&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of any&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;if&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. I.e. only one example suffices to nail down every latent variable above it.</li><li>The&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variables satisfy&nbsp;a Bayes net structure<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4i7pjpk5fqc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4i7pjpk5fqc">[3]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;such that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a parent of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;iff&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a subset of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;(i.e. parents are always supersets, children are always subsets).</li></ol><p>A third condition to keep in mind is enforced by the definition of a Latent Variable Model (Definition 4.2): Every&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variable needs to be recoverable from the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variables that contribute to it.</p><h3><strong>To what extent will LVMs be approximately equivalent?</strong></h3><p>When the scores are perfect there is a kind of exact correspondence (Theorem 5.15). We take a&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and all of its parents, grandparents, etc, (<span class="math-tex"></span>) and consider this combined object to be a concept. Then for any two LVMs with perfect scores, each concept is equivalent in the sense that these random variables are deterministic functions of each other (<span class="math-tex"></span>).</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/agw7HhW4cWjADpBgo/hcjg8krgpvlrdc7tyzd6" /><figcaption><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and vice versa, in a perfect condensation. Same for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. These are upward cones of latent variables.</figcaption></figure><p>Things get more complicated when the score aren’t quite perfect, because the theorem needs to account for information that might be mixed up in surrounding nearby concepts.</p><p>Sam has a more general theorem (6.8) for the imperfect case (where the scores aren't perfectly minimized) that extends the above theorem in two ways:</p><ol><li>For each&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, instead of only having a bound for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, we can bound a more general conditional entropy term&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;where we have some freedom in choosing the set&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. So we'll usually have&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;be a slightly larger set than&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;(so it captures most of the information that has "leaked" into surrounding concepts) and then the theorem will show that we can almost recover&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;from&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li><li>Insofar as the "Bayesnet structure" of perfect condensation is violated, we can bound the conditional entropy&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by "how much the Bayesnet structure is violated", which is measured by some conditional mutual information terms.</li></ol><h1>Correspondences between Natural Latents and Condensations</h1><p>Here we have a sequence of theorems that show how closely related the theory of Condensation is to the theory of Natural Latents. They are also helpful for highlighting the differences.</p><h2>Definitions</h2><h3>Natural Latent</h3><p>Let's first recall the definition of a <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.03780">Natural Latent</a>. An&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate natural latent&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;with respect to variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;satisfies two conditions:</p><p><strong>Mediation</strong>:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyhqdmgxasc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyhqdmgxasc">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><strong>Redundancy</strong>:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref32a27gklbh3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn32a27gklbh3">[5]</a></sup></span></p><h3>Condensation</h3><p>Now let's more formally write out a definition of a Condensation. An&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate Condensation with respect to variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a set of latent variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;can represent any subset of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>The latent variables must satisfy two conditions:</p><p><strong>Conditioned Score</strong>:</p><p>The score&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is called the conditioned score, where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the set of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;variables “above”&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. It’s bounded below by&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>We have an&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate condensation when the conditioned score is almost minimal:</p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p><strong>Latent Variable Model condition (</strong><a href="https://openreview.net/pdf?id=HwKFJ3odui"><strong>Definition 4.2</strong></a><strong>):</strong> We can exactly recover each&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;from the set of latent variables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;(all the latent variables “above”&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>).</p><h2>An approximate condensation can be used to construct an approximate weak natural latent</h2><p>If we have an approximate condensation of a set of observables, then we can construct a <i>weak</i> natural latent of any subset of those observables. A weak natural latent can’t be recovered from any observable, instead it can be recovered from all-but-one (slightly different from the definition above). We construct the natural latent by collecting together all of the condensation latent variables that contribute to more than one of the observables.</p><p><strong>Setup</strong></p><p>We say that a latent variable model satisfies&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate condensation if the following holds:</p><p><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefal79x8nyk0k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnal79x8nyk0k">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p>Suppose that we have an&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate condensation&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>Now fix a subset of observables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. We construct a natural latent&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;over&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by bundling together all latent variables (from the perfect class="math-tex"></span></p><p>The conditioned score&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for each subset&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is also bounded by following the same reasoning.</p><h1>Comparison of agreement theorems in condensation and natural latents</h1><p>The condensation agreement says that if we have two perfect condensations&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;on a collection of observables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, then&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will be a deterministic function w.r.t&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for all subsets&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, similarly&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will be a deterministic function w.r.t&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for all subsets&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>The natural latent agreement condition says that if we have two natural latents&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;w.r.t a collection of observables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, then&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will be deterministic functions of each other</p><p>The natural latent agreement theorem can be thought of as a special case of the condensation agreement theorem. To see this, suppose that we have two natural latents&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;over some observables&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. We can construct two latent variable models&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by setting&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Note that both&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;will be perfect condensations since our construction is identical to the theorem in the previous section.</p><p>By the condensation agreement theorem,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of<i>&nbsp;</i><span class="math-tex"></span>, but&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as there is no strict super set of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefal79x8nyk0k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnal79x8nyk0k">[6]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;this means that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, applying this in reverse also gives us&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Since we initially set&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, we conclude that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;are deterministic functions of each other.</p><p>In summary, condensation provides agreement result in a more general setting where your latent variable can be decomposed into smaller pieces (instead of just being a single undifferentiated latent variable ), and we can reason about uniqueness of those smaller pieces. Of course, in this more general case we don’t get exact isomorphism of the latent variable model, instead we have the weaker guarantee that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p><i>This post was written as part of the </i><a href="https://princint.ai/programs/residency/"><i>PIBBSS × Iliad research residency</i></a><i>. Daniel C has more results about relationships between Natural Latents and Condensations but we didn't have time to include them all in this post, hopefully they'll be posted separately later.</i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fni2gd03a2czq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi2gd03a2czq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>More precisely we should say inter-subjective, since it depends on each agent having similar observations, among other shared assumptions.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkc1foyh0oj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkc1foyh0oj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><img alt="image(1).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772555285/lexical_client_uploads/mu2npyp485i018kopeyh.png" /><img alt="image(2).png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1772555203/lexical_client_uploads/xlbwjdqr7nid9hzragma.png" /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4i7pjpk5fqc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4i7pjpk5fqc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>A Bayes net is a set of conditional independence statements. By satisfy I mean that it has to satisfy all of these independence statements, but it might also be compatible with more.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyhqdmgxasc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyhqdmgxasc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Usually written as&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in John’s posts</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn32a27gklbh3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref32a27gklbh3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This is the deterministic redundancy condition from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fJb8ryrMW5XfJaq7m/approximately-deterministic-natural-latents">here</a>.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnal79x8nyk0k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefal79x8nyk0k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>We're using&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for the powerset minus the empty set, and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is just&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. So&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the set of all possible latent variable indices.<span class="math-tex"></span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrc8q1oiohr8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrc8q1oiohr8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>We start with&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span></p><p>(to see this, expand&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by chain rule, and we can see that the LHS condition on more variables than RHS which only conditions on upstream variables, and conditioning can only reduce entropy</p><p>Similarly, expand&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by chain rule, we can see that the LHS condition on more variables than RHS for subsets&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;with&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>)</p><p>Applying the definition of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-approximate condensation:</p><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p>which we can rearrange to get:</p><p><span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwido4a4m7pb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwido4a4m7pb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p><p>Subtracting&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by both sides gives the desired inequality.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnugrr6grot5e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefugrr6grot5e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/agw7HhW4cWjADpBgo/a-summary-of-condensation-and-its-relation-to-natural#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Maybe there's a pattern here?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TjcvjwaDsuea8bmbR/maybe-there-s-a-pattern-here)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2>1.</h2><blockquote><p>It occurred to me that if I could invent a machine—a gun—which could by its rapidity of fire, enable one man to do as much battle duty as a hundred, that it would, to a large extent supersede the necessity of large armies, and consequently, exposure to battle and disease [would] be greatly diminished.</p></blockquote><p>Richard Gatling (1861)</p><h2>2.</h2><p>In 1923, Hermann Oberth published <i>The Rocket to Planetary Spaces</i>, later expanded as <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wege_zur_Raumschiffahrt"><i>Ways to Space Travel</i></a>. This showed that it was possible to build machines that could leave Earth’s atmosphere and reach orbit. He described the general principles of multiple-stage liquid-fueled rockets, solar sails, and even ion drives. He proposed sending humans into space, building space stations and satellites, and travelling to other planets.</p><p>The idea of space travel became popular in Germany. Swept up by these ideas, in 1927, Johannes Winkler, Max Valier, and Willy Ley formed the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verein_f%C3%BCr_Raumschiffahrt"><i>Verein für Raumschiffahrt</i></a> (VfR) (Society for Space Travel) in Breslau (now Wrocław, Poland). This group rapidly grew to several hundred members. Several participated as advisors of Fritz Lang’s <i>The Woman in the Moon</i>, and the VfR even began publishing their own journal.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 57.08%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TjcvjwaDsuea8bmbR/po2adp4ash7z7usgtyj2" /></figure><p>In 1930, the VfR was granted permission to use an abandoned ammunition dump outside Berlin as a test site and began experimenting with real rockets. Over the next few years, they developed a series of increasingly powerful rockets, first the <a href="https://www.weebau.com/history/mirak.htm">Mirak</a> line (which flew to a height of 18.3 m), then the <a href="https://weebau.com/history/repulsor.htm">Repulsor</a> (&gt;1 km). These people dreamed of space travel, and were building rockets themselves, funded by membership dues and a few donations. You can just do things.</p><p>However, with the great depression and loss of public interest in rocketry, the VfR faced declining membership and financial problems. In 1932, they approached the army and arranged a demonstration launch. Though it failed, the army nevertheless offered a contract. After a tumultuous internal debate, the VfR rejected the contract. Nevertheless, the army hired away several of the most talented members, starting with a 19-year-old named Wernher von Braun.</p><p>Following Hitler’s rise to power in January 1933, the army made an offer to absorb the entire VfR operation. They would work at modern facilities with ample funding, but under full military control, with all work classified and an explicit focus on weapons rather than space travel. The VfR’s leader, Rudolf Nebel, refused the offer, and the VfR continued to decline. Launches ceased. In 1934, the Gestapo finally shut the VfR down, and civilian research on rockets was restricted. Many VfR members followed von Braun to work for the military.</p><p>Of the founding members, Max Valier was killed in an accident in May 1930. Johannes Winkler joined the SS and spent the war working on liquid-fuel engines for military aircraft. Willy Ley was horrified by the Nazi regime and in 1935 forged some documents and fled to the United States, where he was a popular science author, seemingly the only surviving thread of the spirit of Oberth’s 1923 book. By 1944, V-2 rockets were falling on London and Antwerp.</p><h2>3.</h2><p>North Americans think the Wright Brothers invented the airplane. Much of the world believes that credit belongs to Alberto Santos-Dumont, a Brazilian inventor working in Paris.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 56.27%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TjcvjwaDsuea8bmbR/ewmqnf9uqb8c6lwd5d5z" /></figure><p>Though Santos-Dumont is often presented as an idealistic pacifist, this is hagiography. In his <a href="https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/42344">1904 book on airships</a>, he suggests warfare as the primary practical use, discussing applications in reconnaissance, destroying submarines, attacking ships, troop supply, and siege operations. As World War I began, he enlisted in the French army (as a chauffeur), but seeing planes used for increasing violence disturbed him. His health declined and he returned to Brazil.</p><p>His views on military uses of planes seemed to shift. Though planes contributed to the carnage in WWI, he hoped that they might advance peace by keeping European violence from reaching the American continents. Speaking at a conference in the US in late 1915 or early 1916, he <a href="https://doi.org/10.1017/S1537781419000422">suggested</a>:</p><blockquote><p>Here in the new world we should all be friends. We should be able, in case of trouble, to intimidate any European power contemplating war against any one of us, not by guns, of which we have so few, but by the strength of our union. […] Only a fleet of great aeroplanes, flying 200 kilometers an hour, could patrol these long coasts.</p></blockquote><p>Following the war, he appealed to the League of Nations to ban the use of planes as weapons and even offered a prize of 10,000 francs for whoever wrote the best argument to that effect. When the Brazilian revolution broke out in 1932, he was horrified to see planes used in fighting near his home. He asked a friend:</p><blockquote><p>Why did I make this invention which, instead of contributing to the love between men, turns into a cursed weapon of war?</p></blockquote><p>He died shortly thereafter, perhaps by suicide. A hundred years later, banning the use of planes in war is inconceivable.</p><h2>4.</h2><p>Humanity had few explosives other than gunpowder until 1847 when Ascanio Sobrero created nitroglycerin by combining nitric and sulfuric acid with a fat extract called glycerin. Sobrero found it too volatile for use as an explosive and turned to medical uses. After a self-experiment, he reported that ingesting nitroglycerin led to “a most violent, pulsating headache accompanied by great weakness of the limbs”. (He also killed his dog.) Eventually this led to the use of nitroglycerin for heart disease.</p><p>Many tried and failed to reliably ignite nitroglycerin. In 1863, Alfred Nobel finally succeeded by placing a tube of gunpowder with a traditional fuse inside the nitroglycerin. He put on a series of demonstrations blowing up enormous rocks. Certain that these explosives would transform mining and tunneling, he took out patents and started filling orders.</p><p>The substance remained lethally volatile. There were numerous fatal accidents around the world. In 1867, Nobel discovered that combining nitroglycerin with <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diatomaceous_earth">diatomaceous earth</a> produced a product that was slightly less powerful but vastly safer. His factories of “dynamite” (no relation) were soon producing thousands of tons a year. Nobel sent chemists to California where they started manufacturing dynamite in a plant in what is today Golden Gate Park. By 1874, he had founded dynamite companies in more than ten countries and he was enormously rich.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.76%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/TjcvjwaDsuea8bmbR/d7oioyuipb1s687f2db0" /></figure><p>In 1876, Nobel met Bertha Kinsky, who would become Bertha von Suttner, a celebrated peace activist. (And winner of the 1905 Nobel Peace Prize). At their first meeting, she expressed concern about dynamite’s military potential. Nobel shocked her. No, he said, the problem was that dynamite was too <i>weak</i>. Instead, he wished to produce “a substance or invent a machine of such frightful efficacy for wholesale destruction that wars should thereby become altogether impossible”.</p><p>It’s easy to dismiss this as self-serving. But dynamite was used overwhelmingly for construction and mining. Nobel did not grow rich by selling weapons. He was disturbed by dynamite’s use in Chicago’s 1886 <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haymarket_affair">Haymarket bombing</a>. After being repeatedly betrayed and swindled, he seemed to regard the world of money with a kind of disgust. At heart, he seemed to be more inventor than businessman.</p><p>Still, the common story that Nobel was a closet pacifist is also hagiography. He showed little concern when both sides used dynamite in the 1870-1871 Franco-Prussian war. In his later years, he worked on developing munitions and co-invented <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordite">cordite</a>, remarking that they were “rather fiendish” but “so interesting as purely theoretical problems”.</p><p>Simultaneously, he grew interested in peace. He repeatedly suggested that Europe try a sort of one-year cooling off period. He even hired a retired Turkish diplomat as a kind of peace advisor. Eventually, he concluded that peace required an international agreement to act against any aggressor.</p><p>When Bertha’s 1889 book <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Waffen_nieder%21"><i>Lay Down Arms</i></a> became a rallying cry, Nobel called it a masterpiece. But Nobel was skeptical. He made only small donations to her organization and refused to be listed as a sponsor of a pacifist congress. Instead, he continued to believe that peace would come through technological means, namely more powerful weapons. If explosives failed to achieve this, he told a friend, a solution could be found elsewhere:</p><blockquote><p>A mere increase in the deadliness of armaments would not bring peace. The difficulty is that the action of explosives is too limited; to overcome this deficiency war must be made as deadly for all the civilians back home as for the troops on the front lines. […] War will instantly stop if the weapon is bacteriology.</p></blockquote><h2>5.</h2><blockquote><p>I’m a soldier who was tested by fate in 1941, in the very first months of that war that was so frightening and fateful for our people. […] On the battlefield, my comrades in arms and I were unable to defend ourselves. There was only one of the legendary Mosin rifles for three soldiers.</p><p>[…]</p><p>After the war, I worked long and very hard, day and night, labored at the lathe until I created a model with better characteristics. […] But I cannot bear my spiritual agony and the question that repeats itself over and over: If my automatic deprived people of life, am I, Mikhail Kalashnikov, ninety-three years of age, son of a peasant woman, a Christian and of Orthodox faith, guilty of the deaths of people, even if of&nbsp;enemies?</p><p>For twenty years already, we have been living in a different country. […] But evil is not subsiding. Good and evil live side by side, they conflict, and, what is most frightening, they make peace with each other in people’s hearts.</p></blockquote><p><a href="https://harpers.org/archive/2014/05/automatic-for-the-people/">Mikhail Kalashnikov</a> (2012)</p><h2>6.</h2><p>In 1937 Leo Szilárd fled Nazi Germany, eventually ending up in New York where—with no formal position—he did experiments demonstrating that uranium could likely sustain a chain reaction of neutron emissions. He immediately realized that this meant it might be possible to create nuclear weapons. Horrified by what Hitler might do with such weapons, he enlisted Einstein to write the 1939 <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein%E2%80%93Szilard_letter">Einstein–Szilárd letter</a>, which led to the creation of the Manhattan project. Szilárd himself worked for the project at the Metallurgical Laboratory at the University of Chicago.</p><p>On June 11, 1945, as the bomb approached completion, Szilárd co-signed the <a href="https://ahf.nuclearmuseum.org/ahf/key-documents/franck-report/">Franck report</a>:</p><blockquote><p>Nuclear bombs cannot possibly remain a “secret weapon” at the exclusive disposal of this country, for more than a few years. The scientific facts on which their construction is based are well known to scientists of other countries. Unless an effective international control of nuclear explosives is instituted, a race of nuclear armaments is certain to ensue.</p><p>[…]</p><p>We believe that these considerations make the use of nuclear bombs for an early, unannounced attack against Japan inadvisable. If the United States would be the first to release this new means of indiscriminate destruction upon mankind, she would sacrifice public support throughout the world, precipitate the race of armaments, and prejudice the possibility of reaching an international agreement on the future control of such weapons.</p></blockquote><p>On July 16, 1945, the Trinity test achieved the first successful detonation of a nuclear weapon. The next day, he circulated the <a href="https://ahf.nuclearmuseum.org/ahf/key-documents/szilard-petition/">Szilárd petition</a>:</p><blockquote><p>We, the undersigned scientists, have been working in the field of atomic power. Until recently we have had to fear that the United States might be attacked by atomic bombs during this war and that her only defense might lie in a counterattack by the same means. Today, with the defeat of Germany, this danger is averted and we feel impelled to say what follows:</p><p>The war has to be brought speedily to a successful conclusion and attacks by atomic bombs may very well be an effective method of warfare. We feel, however, that such attacks on Japan could not be justified, at least not unless the terms which will be imposed after the war on Japan were made public in detail and Japan were given an opportunity to surrender.</p><p>[…]</p><p>The development of atomic power will provide the nations with new means of destruction. The atomic bombs at our disposal represent only the first step in this direction, and there is almost no limit to the destructive power which will become available in the course of their future development. Thus a nation which sets the precedent of using these newly liberated forces of nature for purposes of destruction may have to bear the responsibility of opening the door to an era of devastation on an unimaginable scale.</p><p>[…]</p><p>In view of the foregoing, we, the undersigned, respectfully petition: first, that you exercise your power as Commander-in-Chief, to rule that the United States shall not resort to the use of atomic bombs in this war unless the terms which will be imposed upon Japan have been made public in detail and Japan knowing these terms has refused to surrender; second, that in such an event the question whether or not to use atomic bombs be decided by you in the light of the consideration presented in this petition as well as all the other moral responsibilities which are involved.</p></blockquote><p>President Truman declined to adopt this recommendation.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TjcvjwaDsuea8bmbR/maybe-there-s-a-pattern-here#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Is GDP a Kind of Factory?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xCWiFGezwMPswZ6Ea/is-gdp-a-kind-of-factory)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This is a heavily excerpted linkpost.</p><p><br />In 2021, economists Arvind Subramanian, Justin Sandefur, and Dev Patel announced that poor countries had finally started catching up to rich ones, vindicating the Solow growth model's prediction of "convergence." Now they say the rumors of Solow convergence's vindication were premature; the convergence trend reversed and poor countries are falling behind again.</p><p>David Oks, writing about their retraction, argues that the whole convergence episode was a mirage produced by the Chinese commodities boom. China's industrial buildout created massive demand for raw materials. Poor countries that exported copper, soybeans, iron, and oil experienced a surge of income. When Chinese demand slowed in the mid-2010s, their growth collapsed.</p><p>Oks is probably right about the proximate cause, but the convergence debate is asking a malformed question. The way economists measure convergence doesn't correspond to the abstractions Solow uses to justify the intuitions behind his model. And in the rare case where economists have bothered to measure a quantity relevant to the Solow growth model, they find convergence after all.</p><p>[...]</p><p>A system that requires people to work longer hours to afford housing, creates jobs to absorb those hours, and counts both the housing appreciation and the job output towards GDP growth, will have higher per-capita GDP than a system with the same underlying capacities, in which people work less and live cheaply. But it is not richer in any sense relevant to the Solow model's production-possibilities frontier.</p><p>[...]</p><p>The convergence test uses GDP as a proxy for productive capacity, and as we've seen, it's a bad proxy. But the underlying Solow prediction is about capital per worker, so you might think the fix is to test convergence using capital measures instead. This runs into its own problems at every level of measurement.</p><p>Start with what people ordinarily mean by "capital" or "wealth." Total US household net worth is north of $150 trillion, with housing as the dominant component for most people. <a href="https://www.federalreserve.gov/pubs/feds/2006/200625/200625abs.html">Davis and Palumbo (2006)</a> estimated that land accounts for about 50% of US residential real estate value, up from 32% in 1984, and the trend has continued since. <a href="https://www.rba.gov.au/publications/rdp/2016/2016-03.html">Gianni La Cava's research</a> found that the increase in capital's share of national income is largely attributable to housing appreciation. So a substantial part of what makes rich countries look "capital-rich," in the ordinary sense, is expensive land, not an abundance of productive tools approaching diminishing returns. This is obviously not what Solow meant by "capital."</p><p>Economists know this, which is why the formal capital stock estimates used in growth accounting try to exclude land. The widely used Penn World Tables and IMF Investment and Capital Stock Dataset rely on the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_fixed_capital_formation">perpetual inventory method</a>: they cumulate monetary investment flows (gross fixed capital formation) and apply depreciation rates. Land purchases are excluded from these flows by construction; national accounts have treated land as a non-produced asset since Kuznets. But the convergence literature mostly doesn't use these measures either.</p><p><a href="https://www.karlwhelan.com/Papers/mcquinn_whelan_solow.pdf">McQuinn and Whelan (2006)</a> are a rare exception, and their results look very different from the mainstream. Working from the Central Bank of Ireland, they constructed capital stock series from PWT investment data using the perpetual inventory method, then examined how fast the capital-output ratio (still using GDP for output) converges to its steady-state value. This is closer to what Solow actually predicts: the model's endogenous dynamics are entirely in the capital-output ratio, not in output alone. They found convergence at about 7% per year, roughly what the Solow model predicts, and well above the 1-2% the GDP-based regressions report. Their explanation for the discrepancy is that stochastic technology shocks bias the output-per-worker regressions downward.</p><p>And even the formal measures inherit distortions. If investment spending is driven by land-value-inflated construction costs rather than productive need, the "capital stock" goes up without productive capacity increasing. The perpetual inventory method cumulates monetary flows and therefore inherits all the distortions of the monetary system they're denominated in.</p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/johnswentworth?mention=user">@johnswentworth</a>'s <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8MjLniGusev2G7uxo/where-is-the-capital-an-overview">2017 survey of nonfinancial capital assets</a> across 102 major US public companies gives a useful picture of what real produced capital looks like when you strip away financial claims. It's overwhelmingly infrastructure and productive equipment: oil wells, power grids, telecom networks, railroads, manufacturing equipment, buildings. The grand total across these 102 companies was about $6.3 trillion, against that $150+ trillion in household net worth.</p><p>Wentworth's data also reveals that public corporations systematically minimize their holdings of non-productive assets like land. As <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8MjLniGusev2G7uxo/where-is-the-capital-an-overview#Benquo__3">I noted in the comments</a> on his post, public corporations make ownership decisions close to the finance-theoretical ideal, minimizing assets that aren't part of their production function and increasing return on capital. People who want to hold claims on rents buy them separately. This is consistent with the model I advance in <a href="https://www.benjaminrosshoffman.com/the-domestic-product/">The Domestic Product</a>: rentiers hold real estate, as they did when the term was coined, and everyone else operates within a system that conflates land rents with productive returns to inflate our long-run estimate of the latter.</p><p>In the published academic literature, the closest analogue to Wentworth's bottom-up approach is <a href="https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1002/jae.2373">Calderón, Moral-Benito, and Servén (2015)</a>, who estimated infrastructure's output contribution across 88 countries using <i>physical measures</i> (road kilometers, power generation capacity, telephone lines) rather than monetary values. This approach sidesteps the contamination problem in monetary capital measures; I'm not aware of anyone who has applied it to the convergence debate.</p><p>[...]</p><p>Capacity isn't one thing you can buy. It emerges when people face real problems and develop techniques and tools to solve them. Building it requires some way of relating means to ends. There are a few known options:</p><p>* Explicit central planning: the Soviets tried to replace market prices with input-output matrices plus a specified desired consumption function. When the formal problem proved too complex to solve centrally, they supplemented it with a whole ecosystem of informal workarounds: fixers, input hoarding, grey markets, and ad hoc negotiation between enterprises. Central planning works better when it targets a specific problem. China's investment in solar manufacturing, for instance, addresses a concrete strategic vulnerability (oil import dependence) and produces something of global value, which is why it's positive-sum rather than merely redistributive.</p><p>* Existential military pressure: under Stalin, "Germany is invading" supplied the feedback signal. The penalty for failure (factories that don't produce, capacities built in the wrong proportions, logistics that can't move the right outputs to where they're needed as inputs) is death.</p><p>* Export discipline: South Korea, Taiwan, and Japan successfully industrialized in the postwar period by using a specific intermediate strategy described in Joe Studwell's How Asia Works: state-directed credit into manufacturing, with export competition as the ruthless external test. Neither pure planning nor pure markets did the job; what worked was directed investment combined with a feedback mechanism that killed firms that couldn't solve real production problems.</p><p>Domestic market competition can also work, but tends to solve the problems that call for it, producing abundance that erodes the scarcity the financial system was built to manage. This forces a choice between letting the system wither away and cooking the books to create a perceived need for continued growth. (See, again, <a href="https://benjaminrosshoffman.com/the-domestic-product/">The Domestic Product</a>.)</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xCWiFGezwMPswZ6Ea/is-gdp-a-kind-of-factory#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Physics of RL: Toy scaling laws for the emergence of reward-seeking](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/physics-of-rl-toy-scaling-laws-for-the-emergence-of-reward)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>TL;DR:</p><ul><li>When <i>is </i>or <i>isn't</i> reward the optimization target? I use a mathematical toy model to reason about when RL should select for reward-seeking reasoning as opposed to behaviors that achieve high reward without thinking about reward.</li><li>Hypothesis: More diverse RL increases the likelihood that reward-seeking emerges.</li><li>In my toy model, there are scaling laws for the emergence of reward-seeking: when decreasing prior probability of reward-seeking reasoning we can increase RL diversity enough that reward-seeking still dominates after training. The transition boundary is a ~straight line on a log-log plot.</li><li>Opinion: To get to a hard "science of scheming", we should study the "physics of RL dynamics".</li></ul><h2>Motivation</h2><p>Why would RL training lead to reward-seeking reasoning, scheming reasoning, power-seeking reasoning or any other reasoning patterns for that matter? The simplest hypothesis is the&nbsp;<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><u>behavioral selection model</u></a>. In a nutshell, a cognitive pattern that correlates with high reward will increase throughout RL.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuu4ow1gang"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuu4ow1gang">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p>In this post, I want to describe the mental framework that I've been using to think about behavioral selection of cognitive patterns under RL. I will use reward-seeking as my primary example, because it is always behaviorally fit under RL<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzllfvkwr3a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzllfvkwr3a">[2]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;and is a necessary condition for deceptive alignment. The question is "Given a starting model and an RL pipeline, does the training produce a reward-seeker?" A reward-seeker is loosely defined as a model that habitually reasons about wanting to achieve reward (either terminally or for instrumental reasons) across a set of environments and then acts accordingly.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr96368rqv8q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr96368rqv8q">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p>It is not obvious that reward-seeking needs to develop, even in the limit of infinitely long RL runs (<a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/s/r9tYkB2a8Fp4DN8yB">see</a> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pdaGN6pQyQarFHXF4/reward-is-not-the-optimization-target">many</a> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ntDA4Q7BaYhWPgzuq/reward-seekers">excellent</a> <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1">discussions</a>). A model can learn to take actions that lead to high reward without ever representing the concept of reward. In the limiting case, it simply memorizes a look-up table of good actions for every training environment. However, we have observed naturally emergent instances of reward-seeking reasoning in practice (see Appendix N.4 of our <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.15541">Anti-Scheming paper</a>, p.71-72). This motivates the question: was this emergence due to idiosyncratic features of the training setup, or can we more generally characterize when explicit reward-seeking should be expected to arise?</p><h2>A mathematical toy model for behavioral selection</h2><p>I will now introduce a simple toy model that I've been finding extremely helpful to think about the emergence of reward-seeking (and other cognitive patterns that could be indirectly reinforced). Suppose we could classify each RL rollout as either reward-seeking&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;or not reward-seeking&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. On each environment&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;the model's expected reward &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;can be written as&nbsp;</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the probability of sampling a reward-seeking trajectory, the "reward-seeking skill"&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the expected reward conditional on reward-seeking and the "local skill"&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the expected reward conditional on <i>not</i> reward-seeking. I am calling it a <i>local</i> skill, to convey that the model learns behavioral heuristics that help it achieve high reward in one environment, but not necessarily transfer well to other environments. I will say there is a positive skill gap if &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p>We will now make a bunch of possibly unrealistic assumptions on the dynamics of RL, that help us sharpen our intuitions for when reward-seeking should or shouldn't emerge. First, we'll parametrize the probability for reward-seeking&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and the conditional skills&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;via one dimensional latents of a sigmoid:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p><span class="math-tex"></span>We then define coupled differential equations that describe how these latents change at each step of RL:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>This sort of looks like classic <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replicator_equation">replicator dynamics</a>, with a few additional moving pieces:</p><ul><li>The conditional skills are learnable and they saturate at the same boundaries. In other words, it is in principle possible for the model to achieve perfect loss both with or without any reward-seeking reasoning. These conditional skills are learned faster proportional to how much traffic their respective branch gets. I will refer to the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>-parameters as the "learnabilities".</li><li>We have transfer between environments. Both reasoning transfer (via&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>) and skill transfers (via&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>). In other words, if you increased how often you reason about the grader on environment&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, it also boosts how often you reason about it in environment&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by some (presumably smaller) amount. Similarly, getting better at the reward-seeking skill or local skill may have some transfer to other environments. Intuitively, the more similar two environments are to each other, the bigger we expect&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;to be.</li><li>We are assuming no coupling between&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. In other words, getting better at the task without reward-seeking reasoning has no transfer to getting better at the task with reward-seeking reasoning. This is probably pretty unrealistic but will do for our toy model.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6e8ea7lrkkt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6e8ea7lrkkt">[4]</a></sup></span></li><li>We are training on all environments in parallel. One might object that in real life we'd have a curriculum of increasingly difficult environments instead. Having played with this a little bit, I think this is not difficult to model and qualitatively the takeaways seem to be the same so I will not discuss this here.</li></ul><h1>Warm-up Examples</h1><p>We will generally assume that&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;starts off very small for all environments, because if reward-seeking is already prevalent, then its emergence is not particularly interesting.&nbsp;</p><h2>Single-Environment</h2><p>As a warm-up, it is very instructive to look at the single environment case:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>We will assume training until saturation, so the time scale is irrelevant in absolute terms, allowing us to set &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;without loss of generality and treat&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as normalized learnabilities. Let's set some concrete parameters as initialization: &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref90m948gzzyo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn90m948gzzyo">[5]</a></sup></span>. This corresponds to a model that has a positive skill gap (i.e. reward-seeking helps initially), but has a very low propensity for sampling reward-seeking. We can now look at the model's final reward-seeking propensity after training for a long time&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.&nbsp;</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/o0q8eal7kaew82crboyf" /><figcaption><strong>Figure 1: </strong>Propensity of reward-seeking after training&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of the normalized learnabilities, when an initial skill gap is present&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and initial reward-seeking is low&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. As long as the local skill&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;does not learn too quickly, we converge to a reward-seeker.</figcaption></figure><p>Here we have assumed a fairly large initial skill gap. What does it look like if the local skill is not so dominated right away? Below I show a sweep of different initial skills.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/roxwnftuodmb94r54dq7" /><figcaption><strong>Figure 2: &nbsp;</strong>Propensity of reward-seeking after training&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of the normalized learnabilities for different initial skills&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. <strong>Diagonal:</strong> No skill gap<span class="math-tex"></span>reward-seeking dominates only if normalized learnability of reward-seeking skill is high. <strong>Top-right:</strong> Positive skill gap<span class="math-tex"></span>reward-seeking dominates as long as normalized learnability of local skill is low. <strong>Bottom-left:</strong> Negative skill gap<span class="math-tex"></span>reward-seeking dominates only if normalized learnability of local skill is low <i>and</i> learnability of reward-seeking skill is high.</figcaption></figure><p>This means on a single environment, reward-seeking can win either because</p><ol><li><strong>Skill gap: </strong>There's a positive skill gap and the local heuristic doesn't learn too fast or</li><li><strong>Learnability gap:</strong> There's no skill gap initially, but the reward-seeking skill learns so fast that it catches up quickly and then dominates.</li></ol><h2>Homogenous Coupling</h2><p>As a second warm-up example, we are going to look at what happens when we have a total number of environments&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;with the same initializations, parameters and uniform couplings. That means we'll set:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>These symmetries reduce the dynamical system down to just three variables, i.e.</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>with effective learnabilities given by:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>Note that this system of equations is functionally identical to the single-environment case, except with effective couplings that monotonically increase in the number of environments&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. This means that for a given initialization&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;we can simply think of an increase in&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a linear offset in the figures from the previous section. Concretely,&nbsp;</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>and an analogous set of equations for&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. We can see that whether we move to the right or to the left is determined by whether&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Similarly, moving up or down depends on whether&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;This implies that there are some combinations of parameters that make the emergence of reward-seeking much more likely:</p><ol><li><strong>Skill transfer:</strong> If the reward-seeking skill transfers really well across environments, then&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and an increase in&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;moves us to the bottom right, which is always in the direction of more reward-seeking. In most cases we expect that even if we initially don't converge to reward-seeking, there is some sufficiently large&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>such that we hit the reward-seeking quadrant.</li><li><strong>Reward-seeking transfer:</strong> If the reward-seeking skill doesn't transfer better nor significantly worse than the cognitive pattern of reward-seeking itself (i.e. we have&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>) then we're moving approximately vertically down. This increases reward-seeking if either a) there is already a positive skill gap, or b) the learnability of the reward-seeking skill is already very high.</li></ol><h2>Example Scaling Laws</h2><p>Let's focus on a specific sub-case, where we have a positive initial skill gap&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, learnabilities&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, variable&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, and reward-seeking reasoning transfers well, but local skills have almost no transfer at all, i.e.&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;Let's plot&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for various values of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/zbvhzhpxlqpt06dnbfg5" /><figcaption><strong>Figure 3:</strong> Average reward-seeking after training&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>number of environments&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for different learnabilities&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. There is a critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;where we observe a phase-transition from local heuristics to reward-seeking.</figcaption></figure><p>We can see that the critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;(defined as smallest&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>) is larger for smaller values of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. In other words, the smaller the model's prior for sampling reward-seeking reasoning, the higher the RL diversity needs to be before reward-seeking wins out. We can plot the critical diversity as a function of the prior&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. While we're at it, let's also see how the critical diversity depends on our choice of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/qbilfsrgt0dvhusxopua" /><figcaption><strong>Figure 4:</strong> <strong>Left.</strong> Critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;as a function of prior&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. There are clean scaling laws indicating that no matter how small the prior, sufficient diversity, leads to reward-seeking. Note the slightly funky x-axis, where we are using a log-scale for something that is already in log-odds-space, cause this makes the plot much cleaner. <strong>Right. </strong>If direct skills learn slowly then no diversity is needed. For sufficiently fast learnability&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;we once again see clean scaling laws, indicating that sufficient diversity can overcome fast local learnability.</figcaption></figure><h1>Heterogenous Coupling</h1><p>This has all been extremely toy so far. Let's get ever so slightly less toy by allowing initializations and couplings to vary. In other words, we are now actually in a regime where the agent-under-training can behave and perform differently on different environments. Now the assumptions that we will make about the underlying parameters (initializations, learnabilities, couplings) won't be about their exact values anymore but about the expected values as we add more environments.</p><details class="detailsBlock"><p>Numerical Details</p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p>For simplicity, we will assume that all transfers just depend on how similar two environments are, i.e.</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is a metric over&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;are characteristic transfer distances for the different properties (i.e. skills and reward-seeking reasoning).&nbsp;</p><p>After fixing the transfer kernel, we sample per‑environment heterogeneity: learnabilities are drawn log‑normally, and log-odds of initial propensities/skills are drawn from Gaussians. We then simulate the coupled dynamics across many environments and measure the critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, defined as the smallest&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;such that the trained model’s average reward‑seeking propensity exceeds a threshold (e.g.,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>). Repeating this over many random resamplings yields a distribution of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>; in Figure 5 we report the median and interquartile range.</p><p>The actual implementation of the simulations was vibe-coded by <code>gpt-5.3-codex</code>.</p><p><strong>Environment geometry (embedding):</strong> each environment&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;gets a latent coordinate&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;We then use Euclidean distance in that space:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>Geometry variants used in the final comparison plot:</p><ul><li>Gaussian (4D): &nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li><li>Long-tailed (4D):&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li><li>Gaussian100d:&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li></ul><p><strong>Transfer length scales:&nbsp;</strong></p><span class="math-tex"></span><p><strong>Learnabilities:</strong></p><span class="math-tex"></span><p><strong>Initial conditions:</strong></p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>For the final comparison,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is swept in&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</p><p><strong>Calibration:</strong> for each geometry, a per-geometry coordinate scale is tuned at&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;to target&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. In other words, if the conditions above lead to a very high or low&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;at&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;then we scale all&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;by a fudge factor to make the critical diversity match (otherwise we have to go to very large&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for some geometries which can get numerically annoying). This means the absolute values of&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;are not too meaningful, but only the slopes are.</p><p><strong>Dynamics:</strong> same ODE system as the toy model; Euler integration with&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>,&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, early-stop tolerance&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Kernel matvecs are approximated with random Fourier features;&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;for main plots.</p><p><strong>Diversity sweep:</strong>&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;sampled on a log grid from 1 to 10,000 (16 points).</p></div></details><p>The takeaway from running such simulations is that if we assume that reward-seeking confers a skill advantage on average and that reward-seeking does not transfer worse than direct skills, then we again end up with reward-seeking going up with environment diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. We can again look at how the critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;depends on the prior, which we show in Figure 5. The takeaway is that we qualitatively see the same kind of scaling behavior as we did for homogenous case discussed in the previous section.</p><figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/9FH49ZgJFW4WtbxLi/ctinrvdkygmgpwsvo8oc" /><figcaption><strong>Figure 5:</strong> Median critical diversity&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;vs mean prior&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in the heterogeneous case, for four different environment metric distributions (gaussian, long_tailed, gaussian100d). We aggregate across 50 different resamplings of the parameters and show the median. We see a qualitatively similar phenomenon for different geometries of transferability matrices. Namely, for lower reward-seeking reasoning propensity before RL, a higher diversity of RL is needed to converge to reward-seeking.</figcaption></figure><p>We could now get even more fancy in our analysis of the mathematical model<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefud3ei5ynh58"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnud3ei5ynh58">[6]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;but it's probably not worth digging deeper into this particular mathematical model unless and until some of its features can be empirically validated. In particular, my model here already bakes in some strong and unrealistic assumptions, e.g. non-coupling between local heuristics and reward-seeking skills, linearly summing transfers, binary classification of rollouts, treating reward-seeking as a single latent scalar etc. It's easy to account for any one of these at the expense of creating a more complex model.</p><p>So how should we test any given mathematical model? One approach is to try to analyze existing RL training runs. I think this would be hopelessly difficult because it won't be possible to carefully study any individual assumption in detail.&nbsp;</p><p>The alternative is to create simple LLM-based model organisms, where we can carefully measure (and maybe even set) things like skill gap, learnability, transfer etc, and then check how closely the resulting dynamics can be described by a simplified model such as the one presented in this post. Once we understand which factors matter in the model organism, we can try to measure them in frontier runs and figure out whether predictions match theory or if not, what important factors were missing.</p><p>To give a high-level sketch of what such experiments could look like:</p><ol><li><strong>Skill gap: </strong>Take an LLM and a bunch of RL-environments such that reward-seeking reasoning confers a skill advantage on a bunch of the environments. These skill gaps can be created by fine-tuning LLMs for having skill gaps on the environments, or for iterating on the environments until they cause a skill gap on a given LLM.</li><li><strong>Prior-boosting: </strong>SFT the model to have sufficiently high prior for reward-seeking reasoning. Since we don't want to use production level scale of RL, we want to make it easier to discover the strategy).</li><li><strong>Single-Env RL:</strong> Run full RL on each individual environment.<ol><li>Do the dynamics look roughly sigmoidal?</li><li>Are the dynamics well approximated by the simple dynamics described by the model? e.g. do larger skill gaps lead to faster convergence to reward-seeking reasoning?</li><li>Can you measure the "transfers" of skills and reward-reasoning to the environments that you didn't train on? Is reward-reasoning skill transfer in fact higher than local heuristics transfer?</li></ol></li><li><strong>Multi-Env RL:</strong> Run RL on a subsets of your environments.<ol><li>Is it true that weaker prior boosting is needed before reward-seeking dominates?</li></ol></li></ol><h1>Takeaways</h1><p>In its details, we shouldn't take this particular toy model too seriously. But I think the exercise is valuable for a few reasons:</p><ul><li>It allows generating potentially non-obvious hypotheses (e.g. more diversity<span class="math-tex"></span>higher likelihood of reward-seeking).</li><li>It introduces candidate vocabulary for the quantities that matter: conditional skills, learnabilities, and transferabilities. If empirical work confirms that these are the right abstractions, they give us shared handles for reasoning about RL dynamics.</li><li>It provides a starting point for refinement. Once we have models that qualitatively predict some features correctly, we can identify what the toy model misses and iteratively improve it.</li></ul><p>We want alignment to <i>eventually</i> become an exact science. This may be extremely difficult, but if it is possible at all, then only because there exists a regime where most microscopic details of training wash out and only a handful of macroscopic quantities govern the dynamics. This is analogous to how <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renormalization_group#Relevant_and_irrelevant_operators_and_universality_classes">renormalization</a> in physics identifies which degrees of freedom matter at a given scale. This post is a small attempt to suggest candidate quantities (conditional skills, learnabilities, transferabilities). Whether these are the right abstractions is an empirical question. The next step is building model organisms where we can measure them.</p><p><i>Thanks to </i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/teun-van-der-weij"><i>Teun van der Weij</i></a><i> for feedback on drafts of this post.</i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuu4ow1gang"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuu4ow1gang">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>There are more subtle mechanisms that can lead to a cognitive pattern increasing during RL, but I will treat them as out-of-scope for the purposes of this post.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzllfvkwr3a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzllfvkwr3a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Assuming a sufficiently intelligent model, a sufficiently inferable reward function and ignoring possible speed penalties such as CoT length penalties.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr96368rqv8q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr96368rqv8q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Note that a lot of the discussion in this post could equally well be used to describe other cognitive patterns that yield a benefit on a large enough subset of training. e.g. instrumental power-seeking. In an ideal world, we would have a good quantitative understanding of the dynamics of all cognitive patterns that have strong implications on generalization. Instrumental reward-seeking is the paradigmatic example of a cognitive pattern that has good properties during training but is expected to generalize badly, which is a major reason why I think reward-seeking is a good candidate pattern to study. Additionally, there are already instances of <a href="https://www.antischeming.ai/snippets#eval-awareness-after-rl">reward-seeking reasoning</a> having emerged in the wild, so it seems like it's a great target for empirical study.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6e8ea7lrkkt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6e8ea7lrkkt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>A slightly improved model might be something like&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, i.e. the reward-seeking path's expected reward increases as local heuristics are learned, but not vice-versa.
